[Narrator] It took Jude more than by surprise that the man with the soul was thus and thus; and he felt that he could not open up his confidences. [/]
[Narrator] They talked a little longer, but constrainedly, for when the musician found that Jude was a poor man his manner changed from what it had been while Jude ’s appearance and address deceived him as to his position and pursuits. [/]
[Narrator] Jude stammered out something about his feelings in wishing to congratulate the author on such an exalted composition, and took an embarrassed leave. [/]
[Narrator] All the way home by the slow Sunday train, sitting in the fireless waiting - rooms on this cold spring day, he was depressed enough at his simplicity in taking such a journey. [/]
[Narrator] But no sooner did he reach his Melchester lodging than he found awaiting him a letter which had arrived that morning a few minutes after he had left the house. [/]
[Narrator] It was a contrite little note from Sue, in which she said, with sweet humility, that she felt she had been horrid in telling him he was not to come to see her, that she despised herself for having been so conventional; and that he was to be sure to come by the eleven - forty - five train that very Sunday, and have dinner with them at half - past one. [/]
[Narrator] Jude almost tore his hair at having missed this letter till it was too late to act upon its contents; but he had chastened himself considerably of late, and at last his chimerical expedition to Kennetbridge really did seem to have been another special intervention of Providence to keep him away from temptation. [/]
[Narrator] But a growing impatience of faith, which he had noticed in himself more than once of late, made him pass over in ridicule the idea that God sent people on fools ’ errands. [/]
[Narrator] He longed to see her; he was angry at having missed her: and he wrote instantly, telling her what had happened, and saying he had not enough patience to wait till the following Sunday, but would come any day in the week that she liked to name. [/]
[Narrator] Since he wrote a little over - ardently, Sue, as her manner was, delayed her reply till Thursday before Good Friday, when she said he might come that afternoon if he wished, this being the earliest day on which she could welcome him, for she was now assistant - teacher in her husband ’s school. [/]
[Narrator] Jude therefore got leave from the cathedral works at the trifling expense of a stoppage of pay, and went. [/]
[Shaston] Part Fourth AT SHASTON _ “ Whoso prefers either Matrimony or other Ordinance before the Good of Man and the plain Exigence of Charity, let him profess Papist, or Protestant, or what he will, he is no better than a Pharisee. ”_—J. MILTON. [/]
[Narrator] I Shaston, the ancient British Palladour, From whose foundation first such strange reports arise, (as Drayton sang it), was, and is, in itself the city of a dream. [/]
[Narrator] Vague imaginings of its castle, its three mints, its magnificent apsidal abbey, the chief glory of South Wessex, its twelve churches, its shrines, chantries, hospitals, its gabled freestone mansions — all now ruthlessly swept away — throw the visitor, even against his will, into a pensive melancholy, which the stimulating atmosphere and limitless landscape around him can scarcely dispel. [/]
[Narrator] The spot was the burial - place of a king and a queen, of abbots and abbesses, saints and bishops, knights and squires. [/]
[KingEdward] The bones of King Edward “ the Martyr, ” carefully removed hither for holy preservation, brought Shaston a renown which made it the resort of pilgrims from every part of Europe, and enabled it to maintain a reputation extending far beyond English shores. [/]
[Narrator] To this fair creation of the great Middle - Age the Dissolution was, as historians tell us, the death - knell. [/]
[Narrator] With the destruction of the enormous abbey the whole place collapsed in a general ruin: the Martyr ’s bones met with the fate of the sacred pile that held them, and not a stone is now left to tell where they lie. [/]
[Narrator] The natural picturesqueness and singularity of the town still remain; but strange to say these qualities, which were noted by many writers in ages when scenic beauty is said to have been unappreciated, are passed over in this, and one of the queerest and quaintest spots in England stands virtually unvisited to - day. [/]
[Narrator] It has a unique position on the summit of a steep and imposing scarp, rising on the north, south, and west sides of the borough out of the deep alluvial Vale of Blackmoor, the view from the Castle Green over three counties of verdant pasture — South, Mid, and Nether Wessex — being as sudden a surprise to the unexpectant traveller ’s eyes as the medicinal air is to his lungs. [/]
[Narrator] Impossible to a railway, it can best be reached on foot, next best by light vehicles; and it is hardly accessible to these but by a sort of isthmus on the north - east, that connects it with the high chalk table - land on that side. [/]
[Narrator] Such is, and such was, the now world - forgotten Shaston or Palladour. [/]
[Narrator] Its situation rendered water the great want of the town; and within living memory, horses, donkeys and men may have been seen toiling up the winding ways to the top of the height, laden with tubs and barrels filled from the wells beneath the mountain, and hawkers retailing their contents at the price of a halfpenny a bucketful. [/]
[Narrator] This difficulty in the water supply, together with two other odd facts, namely, that the chief graveyard slopes up as steeply as a roof behind the church, and that in former times the town passed through a curious period of corruption, conventual and domestic, gave rise to the saying that Shaston was remarkable for three consolations to man, such as the world afforded not elsewhere. [/]
[Narrator] It was a place where the churchyard lay nearer heaven than the church steeple, where beer was more plentiful than water, and where there were more wanton women than honest wives and maids. [/]
[Narrator] It is also said that after the Middle Ages the inhabitants were too poor to pay their priests, and hence were compelled to pull down their churches, and refrain altogether from the public worship of God; a necessity which they bemoaned over their cups in the settles of their inns on Sunday afternoons. [/]
[Narrator] In those days the Shastonians were apparently not without a sense of humour. [/]
[Narrator] There was another peculiarity — this a modern one — which Shaston appeared to owe to its site. [/]
[Narrator] It was the resting - place and headquarters of the proprietors of wandering vans, shows, shooting - galleries, and other itinerant concerns, whose business lay largely at fairs and markets. [/]
[Narrator] As strange wild birds are seen assembled on some lofty promontory, meditatively pausing for longer flights, or to return by the course they followed thither, so here, in this cliff - town, stood in stultified silence the yellow and green caravans bearing names not local, as if surprised by a change in the landscape so violent as to hinder their further progress; and here they usually remained all the winter till they turned to seek again their old tracks in the following spring. [/]
[Narrator] It was to this breezy and whimsical spot that Jude ascended from the nearest station for the first time in his life about four o’clock one afternoon, and entering on the summit of the peak after a toilsome climb, passed the first houses of the aerial town; and drew towards the school - house. [/]
[Narrator] The hour was too early; the pupils were still in school, humming small, like a swarm of gnats; and he withdrew a few steps along Abbey Walk, whence he regarded the spot which fate had made the home of all he loved best in the world. [/]
[Narrator] In front of the schools, which were extensive and stone - built, grew two enormous beeches with smooth mouse - coloured trunks, as such trees will only grow on chalk uplands. [/]
[Narrator] Within the mullioned and transomed windows he could see the black, brown, and flaxen crowns of the scholars over the sills, and to pass the time away he walked down to the level terrace where the abbey gardens once had spread, his heart throbbing in spite of him. [/]
[Narrator] Unwilling to enter till the children were dismissed he remained here till young voices could be heard in the open air, and girls in white pinafores over red and blue frocks appeared dancing along the paths which the abbess, prioress, subprioress, and fifty nuns had demurely paced three centuries earlier. [/]
[Narrator] Retracing his steps he found that he had waited too long, and that Sue had gone out into the town at the heels of the last scholar, Mr. Phillotson having been absent all the afternoon at a teachers ’ meeting at Shottsford. [/]
[Narrator] Jude went into the empty schoolroom and sat down, the girl who was sweeping the floor having informed him that Mrs. Phillotson would be back again in a few minutes. [/]
[Narrator] A piano stood near — actually the old piano that Phillotson had possessed at Marygreen — and though the dark afternoon almost prevented him seeing the notes Jude touched them in his humble way, and could not help modulating into the hymn which had so affected him in the previous week. [/]
[Narrator] A figure moved behind him, and thinking it was still the girl with the broom Jude took no notice, till the person came close and laid her fingers lightly upon his bass hand. [/]
[Narrator] The imposed hand was a little one he seemed to know, and he turned. [/]
[Sue] “ Do n’t stop, ” said Sue. [/]
[Sue] “ I like it. [/]
[Sue] I learnt it before I left Melchester. [/]
[Sue] They used to play it in the training school. ” [/]

[Sue] Play it for me. ” [/]

[Narrator] Jude grasped it — just as he had done before her marriage. [/]

[Sue] “ Oh, you goose — to do just what I should have done! [/]
[Narrator] Why did you? ” “ Because we are not alike, ” he said drily. [/]

[Sue] It is no trouble to get the kettle and things brought in. [/]

[Sue] It is so antique and dismal that it depresses me dreadfully. [/]
[Sue] Such houses are very well to visit, but not to live in — I feel crushed into the earth by the weight of so many previous lives there spent. [/]
[Sue] In a new place like these schools there is only your own life to support. [/]
[Sue] Sit down, and I ’ll tell Ada to bring the tea - things across. ” [/]
[Narrator] He waited in the light of the stove, the door of which she flung open before going out, and when she returned, followed by the maiden with tea, they sat down by the same light, assisted by the blue rays of a spirit - lamp under the brass kettle on the stand. [/]
[Sue] “ This is one of your wedding - presents to me, ” she said, signifying the latter. [/]
[Jude] “ Yes, ” said Jude. [/]
[Jude] The kettle of his gift sang with some satire in its note, to his mind; and to change the subject he said, “ Do you know of any good readable edition of the uncanonical books of the New Testament? [/]
[Narrator] You do n’t read them in the school I suppose? ” “ Oh dear no! —’twould alarm the neighbourhood … Yes, there is one. [/]
[I] I am not familiar with it now, though I was interested in it when my former friend was alive. [/]
[Narrator] Cowper ’s _ Apocryphal Gospels _. ” “ That sounds like what I want. ” [/]

[Jude] He wondered if she talked of him to Phillotson. [/]

[I] “ It is quite like the genuine article. [/]

[He] “ Sue, I sometimes think you are a flirt, ” said he abruptly. [/]

[Narrator] she said, the tragic contralto note having come back as of old. [/]
[I] “ It is getting too dark to stay together like this, after playing morbid Good Friday tunes that make one feel what one should n’t! [/]
[I] … We must n’t sit and talk in this way any more. [/]
[I] Yes — you must go away, for you mistake me! [/]

[Narrator] Strange that his first aspiration — towards academical proficiency — had been checked by a woman, and that his second aspiration — towards apostleship — had also been checked by a woman. [/]
[He] “ Is it, ” he said, “ that the women are to blame; or is it the artificial system of things, under which the normal sex - impulses are turned into devilish domestic gins and springs to noose and hold back those who want to progress? ” It had been his standing desire to become a prophet, however humble, to his struggling fellow - creatures, without any thought of personal gain. [Narrator] VI In returning to his native town of Shaston as schoolmaster Phillotson had won the interest and awakened the memories of the inhabitants, who, though they did not honour him for his miscellaneous acquirements as he would have been honoured elsewhere, retained for him a sincere regard. [/]
[Narrator] When, shortly after his arrival, he brought home a pretty wife — awkwardly pretty for him, if he did not take care, they said — they were glad to have her settle among them. [/]
[Narrator] For some time after her flight from that home Sue ’s absence did not excite comment. [/]
[Narrator] Her place as monitor in the school was taken by another young woman within a few days of her vacating it, which substitution also passed without remark, Sue ’s services having been of a provisional nature only. [/]
[Narrator] When, however, a month had passed, and Phillotson casually admitted to an acquaintance that he did not know where his wife was staying, curiosity began to be aroused; till, jumping to conclusions, people ventured to affirm that Sue had played him false and run away from him. [/]
[Narrator] The schoolmaster ’s growing languor and listlessness over his work gave countenance to the idea. [/]
[Narrator] Though Phillotson had held his tongue as long as he could, except to his friend Gillingham, his honesty and directness would not allow him to do so when misapprehensions as to Sue ’s conduct spread abroad. [/]
[Narrator] On a Monday morning the chairman of the school committee called, and after attending to the business of the school drew Phillotson aside out of earshot of the children. [/]
[TheChairman] “ You ’ll excuse my asking, Phillotson, since everybody is talking of it: is this true as to your domestic affairs — that your wife ’s going away was on no visit, but a secret elopement with a lover? [/]
[TheChairman] If so, I condole with you. ” [/]
[Phillotson] “ Do n’t, ” said Phillotson. [/]
[Phillotson] “ There was no secret about it. ” [/]
[Narrator] “ She has gone to visit friends? ” “ No. ” “ Then what has happened? ” “ She has gone away under circumstances that usually call for condolence with the husband. [/]
[Phillotson] But I gave my consent. ” [/]
[Narrator] The chairman looked as if he had not apprehended the remark. [/]
[Phillotson] “ What I say is quite true, ” Phillotson continued testily. [/]
[Phillotson] “ She asked leave to go away with her lover, and I let her. [/]
[Phillotson] Why should n’t I? [/]
[Phillotson] A woman of full age, it was a question of her own conscience — not for me. [/]
[Phillotson] I was not her gaoler. [/]
[Phillotson] I ca n’t explain any further. [/]
[Phillotson] I do n’t wish to be questioned. ” [/]
[Narrator] The children observed that much seriousness marked the faces of the two men, and went home and told their parents that something new had happened about Mrs. Phillotson. [/]
[Narrator] Then Phillotson ’s little maidservant, who was a schoolgirl just out of her standards, said that Mr. Phillotson had helped in his wife ’s packing, had offered her what money she required, and had written a friendly letter to her young man, telling him to take care of her. [/]
[Narrator] The chairman of committee thought the matter over, and talked to the other managers of the school, till a request came to Phillotson to meet them privately. [/]
[Narrator] The meeting lasted a long time, and at the end the school - master came home, looking as usual pale and worn. [/]
[Narrator] Gillingham was sitting in his house awaiting him. [/]
[Phillotson] “ Well; it is as you said, ” observed Phillotson, flinging himself down wearily in a chair. [/]
[Phillotson] “ They have requested me to send in my resignation on account of my scandalous conduct in giving my tortured wife her liberty — or, as they call it, condoning her adultery. [/]
[Phillotson] But I sha n’t resign! ” [/]
[Narrator] “ I think I would. ” “ I wo n’t. [/]
[Phillotson] It is no business of theirs. [/]
[Phillotson] It does n’t affect me in my public capacity at all. [/]
[Phillotson] They may expel me if they like. ” [/]
[Gillingham] “ If you make a fuss it will get into the papers, and you ’ll never get appointed to another school. [/]
[Gillingham] You see, they have to consider what you did as done by a teacher of youth — and its effects as such upon the morals of the town; and, to ordinary opinion, your position is indefensible. [/]
[Gillingham] You must let me say that. ” [/]
[Narrator] To this good advice, however, Phillotson would not listen. [/]
[Phillotson] “ I do n’t care, ” he said. [/]
[Phillotson] “ I do n’t go unless I am turned out. [/]
[Phillotson] And for this reason; that by resigning I acknowledge I have acted wrongly by her; when I am more and more convinced every day that in the sight of Heaven and by all natural, straightforward humanity, I have acted rightly. ” [/]
[Narrator] Gillingham saw that his rather headstrong friend would not be able to maintain such a position as this; but he said nothing further, and in due time — indeed, in a quarter of an hour — the formal letter of dismissal arrived, the managers having remained behind to write it after Phillotson ’s withdrawal. [/]
[Narrator] The latter replied that he should not accept dismissal; and called a public meeting, which he attended, although he looked so weak and ill that his friend implored him to stay at home. [/]
[Narrator] When he stood up to give his reasons for contesting the decision of the managers he advanced them firmly, as he had done to his friend, and contended, moreover, that the matter was a domestic theory which did not concern them. [/]
[Narrator] This they over - ruled, insisting that the private eccentricities of a teacher came quite within their sphere of control, as it touched the morals of those he taught. [/]
[Narrator] Phillotson replied that he did not see how an act of natural charity could injure morals. [/]
[Narrator] All the respectable inhabitants and well - to - do fellow - natives of the town were against Phillotson to a man. [/]
[Narrator] But, somewhat to his surprise, some dozen or more champions rose up in his defence as from the ground. [/]
[Narrator] It has been stated that Shaston was the anchorage of a curious and interesting group of itinerants, who frequented the numerous fairs and markets held up and down Wessex during the summer and autumn months. [/]
[Narrator] Although Phillotson had never spoken to one of these gentlemen they now nobly led the forlorn hope in his defence. [/]
[ATestYourStrengthMan] The body included two cheap Jacks, a shooting - gallery proprietor and the ladies who loaded the guns, a pair of boxing - masters, a steam - roundabout manager, two travelling broom - makers, who called themselves widows, a gingerbread - stall keeper, a swing - boat owner, and a “ test - your - strength ” man. [/]
[Narrator] This generous phalanx of supporters, and a few others of independent judgment, whose own domestic experiences had been not without vicissitude, came up and warmly shook hands with Phillotson; after which they expressed their thoughts so strongly to the meeting that issue was joined, the result being a general scuffle, wherein a black board was split, three panes of the school windows were broken, an inkbottle was spilled over a town - councillor ’s shirt front, a churchwarden was dealt such a topper with the map of Palestine that his head went right through Samaria, and many black eyes and bleeding noses were given, one of which, to everybody ’s horror, was the venerable incumbent ’s, owing to the zeal of an emancipated chimney - sweep, who took the side of Phillotson ’s party. [/]
[Narrator] When Phillotson saw the blood running down the rector ’s face he deplored almost in groans the untoward and degrading circumstances, regretted that he had not resigned when called upon, and went home so ill that next morning he could not leave his bed. [/]
[Narrator] The farcical yet melancholy event was the beginning of a serious illness for him; and he lay in his lonely bed in the pathetic state of mind of a middle - aged man who perceives at length that his life, intellectual and domestic, is tending to failure and gloom. [/]
[Narrator] Gillingham came to see him in the evenings, and on one occasion mentioned Sue ’s name. [/]
[Phillotson] “ She does n’t care anything about me! ” said Phillotson. [/]
[Narrator] “ Why should she? ” “ She does n’t know you are ill. ” [/]
[Narrator] “ So much the better for both of us. ” “ Where are her lover and she living? ” “ At Melchester — I suppose; at least he was living there some time ago. ” [/]
[Narrator] When Gillingham reached home he sat and reflected, and at last wrote an anonymous line to Sue, on the bare chance of its reaching her, the letter being enclosed in an envelope addressed to Jude at the diocesan capital. [/]
[Narrator] Arriving at that place it was forwarded to Marygreen in North Wessex, and thence to Aldbrickham by the only person who knew his present address — the widow who had nursed his aunt. [/]
[Narrator] Three days later, in the evening, when the sun was going down in splendour over the lowlands of Blackmoor, and making the Shaston windows like tongues of fire to the eyes of the rustics in that vale, the sick man fancied that he heard somebody come to the house, and a few minutes after there was a tap at the bedroom door. [/]
[Narrator] Phillotson did not speak; the door was hesitatingly opened, and there entered — Sue. [/]
[Narrator] She was in light spring clothing, and her advent seemed ghostly — like the flitting in of a moth. [/]
[Narrator] He turned his eyes upon her, and flushed; but appeared to check his primary impulse to speak. [/]
[Sue] “ I have no business here, ” she said, bending her frightened face to him. [/]
[Narrator] “ But I heard you were ill — very ill; and — and as I know that you recognize other feelings between man and woman than physical love, I have come. ” “ I am not very ill, my dear friend. [/]
[Narrator] Only unwell. ” “ I did n’t know that; and I am afraid that only a severe illness would have justified my coming! ” [/]
[Phillotson] “ Yes … yes. [/]
[Phillotson] And I almost wish you had not come! [/]
[Phillotson] It is a little too soon — that ’s all I mean. [/]
[Phillotson] Still, let us make the best of it. [/]
[Narrator] You have n’t heard about the school, I suppose? ” “ No — what about it? ” “ Only that I am going away from here to another place. [/]

[Narrator] Sue did not for a moment, either now or later, suspect what troubles had resulted to him from letting her go; it never once seemed to cross her mind, and she had received no news whatever from Shaston. [/]

[Sue] “ They are mostly beautiful from here, owing to the rays crossing the mist of the vale. [/]

[Sue] “ But see how I mean. ” [/]

[Narrator] she said. [/]
[Sue] “ And I am sure it will cheer you — I do so hope it will! ” [/]
[Narrator] She spoke with a childlike, repentant kindness, as if she could not do too much for him. [/]
[Narrator] Phillotson smiled sadly. [/]

[Narrator] he murmured as the sun glowed in his eyes. [/]

[Narrator] It grew dusk quickly in the gloomy room during their intermittent chat, and when candles were brought and it was time to leave she put her hand in his or rather allowed it to flit through his; for she was significantly light in touch. [/]

[Phillotson] “ She ’s his — his from lips to heel! ” said Phillotson; but so faintly that in closing the door she did not hear it. [/]
[Narrator] The dread of a reactionary change in the schoolmaster ’s sentiments, coupled, perhaps, with a faint shamefacedness at letting even him know what a slipshod lack of thoroughness, from a man ’s point of view, characterized her transferred allegiance, prevented her telling him of her, thus far, incomplete relations with Jude; and Phillotson lay writhing like a man in hell as he pictured the prettily dressed, maddening compound of sympathy and averseness who bore his name, returning impatiently to the home of her lover. [Narrator] Gillingham was so interested in Phillotson ’s affairs, and so seriously concerned about him, that he walked up the hill - side to Shaston two or three times a week, although, there and back, it was a journey of nine miles, which had to be performed between tea and supper, after a hard day ’s work in school. [/]
[Narrator] When he called on the next occasion after Sue ’s visit his friend was downstairs, and Gillingham noticed that his restless mood had been supplanted by a more fixed and composed one. [/]
[Phillotson] “ She ’s been here since you called last, ” said Phillotson. [/]
[Gillingham] “ Not Mrs. Phillotson? ” “ Yes. ” [/]
[Gillingham] “ Ah! [/]
[Gillingham] You have made it up? ” “ No … She just came, patted my pillow with her little white hand, played the thoughtful nurse for half an hour, and went away. ” [/]
[Gillingham] “ Well — I’m hanged! [/]
[Gillingham] A little hussy! ” [/]
[Gillingham] “ What do you say? ” “ Oh — nothing! ” [/]
[Gillingham] “ What do you mean? ” “ I mean, what a tantalizing, capricious little woman! [/]
[Gillingham] If she were not your wife — ” “ She is not; she ’s another man ’s except in name and law. [/]
[Gillingham] And I have been thinking — it was suggested to me by a conversation I had with her — that, in kindness to her, I ought to dissolve the legal tie altogether; which, singularly enough, I think I can do, now she has been back, and refused my request to stay after I said I had forgiven her. [/]
[Gillingham] I believe that fact would afford me opportunity of doing it, though I did not see it at the moment. [/]
[Gillingham] What ’s the use of keeping her chained on to me if she does n’t belong to me? [/]
[Gillingham] I know — I feel absolutely certain — that she would welcome my taking such a step as the greatest charity to her. [/]
[Gillingham] For though as a fellow - creature she sympathizes with, and pities me, and even weeps for me, as a husband she can not endure me — she loathes me — there ’s no use in mincing words — she loathes me, and my only manly, and dignified, and merciful course is to complete what I have begun … And for worldly reasons, too, it will be better for her to be independent. [/]
[Gillingham] I have hopelessly ruined my prospects because of my decision as to what was best for us, though she does not know it; I see only dire poverty ahead from my feet to the grave; for I can be accepted as teacher no more. [/]
[Gillingham] I shall probably have enough to do to make both ends meet during the remainder of my life, now my occupation ’s gone; and I shall be better able to bear it alone. [/]
[Gillingham] I may as well tell you that what has suggested my letting her go is some news she brought me — the news that Fawley is doing the same. ” [/]
[Gillingham] “ Oh — he had a spouse, too? [/]
[Gillingham] A queer couple, these lovers! ” [/]
[Gillingham] “ Well — I do n’t want your opinion on that. [/]
[Gillingham] What I was going to say is that my liberating her can do her no possible harm, and will open up a chance of happiness for her which she has never dreamt of hitherto. [/]
[Gillingham] For then they ’ll be able to marry, as they ought to have done at first. ” [/]
[Narrator] Gillingham did not hurry to reply. [/]
[Gillingham] “ I may disagree with your motive, ” he said gently, for he respected views he could not share. [/]
[Gillingham] “ But I think you are right in your determination — if you can carry it out. [/]
[Gillingham] I doubt, however, if you can. ” [/]
[I] Part Fifth AT ALDBRICKHAM AND ELSEWHERE _ “ Thy aerial part, and all the fiery parts which are mingled in thee, though by nature they have an upward tendency, still in obedience to the disposition of the universe they are over - powered here in the compound mass the body. ”_—M. [/]
[I] ANTONINUS (Long). [/]
[Narrator] I How Gillingham ’s doubts were disposed of will most quickly appear by passing over the series of dreary months and incidents that followed the events of the last chapter, and coming on to a Sunday in the February of the year following. [/]
[Narrator] Sue and Jude were living in Aldbrickham, in precisely the same relations that they had established between themselves when she left Shaston to join him the year before. [/]
[Narrator] The proceedings in the law - courts had reached their consciousness, but as a distant sound and an occasional missive which they hardly understood. [/]
[Narrator] They had met, as usual, to breakfast together in the little house with Jude ’s name on it, that he had taken at fifteen pounds a year, with three - pounds - ten extra for rates and taxes, and furnished with his aunt ’s ancient and lumbering goods, which had cost him about their full value to bring all the way from Marygreen. [/]
[Narrator] Sue kept house, and managed everything. [/]
[Narrator] As he entered the room this morning Sue held up a letter she had just received. [/]
[Jude] “ Well; and what is it about? ” [/]
[Narrator] he said after kissing her. [/]
[Jude] “ That the decree _ nisi _ in the case of Phillotson _ versus _ Phillotson and Fawley, pronounced six months ago, has just been made absolute. ” “ Ah, ” said Jude, as he sat down. [/]
[Narrator] The same concluding incident in Jude ’s suit against Arabella had occurred about a month or two earlier. [/]
[Narrator] Both cases had been too insignificant to be reported in the papers, further than by name in a long list of other undefended cases. [/]
[Jude] “ Now then, Sue, at any rate, you can do what you like! ” [/]
[Narrator] He looked at his sweetheart curiously. [/]
[Jude] “ Are we — you and I — just as free now as if we had never married at all? ” “ Just as free — except, I believe, that a clergyman may object personally to remarry you, and hand the job on to somebody else. ” [/]
[Jude] “ But I wonder — do you think it is really so with us? [/]
[Jude] I know it is generally. [/]
[Jude] But I have an uncomfortable feeling that my freedom has been obtained under false pretences! ” [/]
[Jude] “ How? ” “ Well — if the truth about us had been known, the decree would n’t have been pronounced. [/]
[Jude] It is only, is it, because we have made no defence, and have led them into a false supposition? [/]
[Narrator] Therefore is my freedom lawful, however proper it may be? ” “ Well — why did you let it be under false pretences? [/]
[He] You have only yourself to blame, ” he said mischievously. [/]
[Jude] “ Jude — don’t! [/]
[Jude] You ought not to be touchy about that still. [/]
[Jude] You must take me as I am. ” [/]
[Jude] “ Very well, darling: so I will. [/]
[Jude] Perhaps you were right. [/]
[Jude] As to your question, we were not obliged to prove anything. [/]
[Jude] That was their business. [/]
[Jude] Anyhow we are living together. ” [/]
[Jude] “ Yes. [/]
[Jude] Though not in their sense. ” “ One thing is certain, that however the decree may be brought about, a marriage is dissolved when it is dissolved. [/]
[Jude] There is this advantage in being poor obscure people like us — that these things are done for us in a rough and ready fashion. [/]
[Jude] It was the same with me and Arabella. [/]
[Jude] I was afraid her criminal second marriage would have been discovered, and she punished; but nobody took any interest in her — nobody inquired, nobody suspected it. [/]
[Jude] If we ’d been patented nobilities we should have had infinite trouble, and days and weeks would have been spent in investigations. ” [/]
[Narrator] By degrees Sue acquired her lover ’s cheerfulness at the sense of freedom, and proposed that they should take a walk in the fields, even if they had to put up with a cold dinner on account of it. [/]
[Narrator] Jude agreed, and Sue went up - stairs and prepared to start, putting on a joyful coloured gown in observance of her liberty; seeing which Jude put on a lighter tie. [/]
[Jude] “ Now we ’ll strut arm and arm, ” he said, “ like any other engaged couple. [/]
[Jude] We ’ve a legal right to. ” [/]
[Narrator] They rambled out of the town, and along a path over the low - lying lands that bordered it, though these were frosty now, and the extensive seed - fields were bare of colour and produce. [/]
[Narrator] The pair, however, were so absorbed in their own situation that their surroundings were little in their consciousness. [/]
[I] “ Well, my dearest, the result of all this is that we can marry after a decent interval. ” [/]
[Sue] “ Yes; I suppose we can, ” said Sue, without enthusiasm. [/]
[I] “ And are n’t we going to? ” “ I do n’t like to say no, dear Jude; but I feel just the same about it now as I have done all along. [/]
[I] I have just the same dread lest an iron contract should extinguish your tenderness for me, and mine for you, as it did between our unfortunate parents. ” [/]
[I] “ Still, what can we do? [/]
[I] I do love you, as you know, Sue. ” [/]
[I] “ I know it abundantly. [/]
[I] But I think I would much rather go on living always as lovers, as we are living now, and only meeting by day. [/]
[I] It is so much sweeter — for the woman at least, and when she is sure of the man. [/]
[I] And henceforward we need n’t be so particular as we have been about appearances. ” [/]
[You] “ Our experiences of matrimony with others have not been encouraging, I own, ” said he, with some gloom; “ either owing to our own dissatisfied, unpractical natures, or by our misfortune. [/]
[Narrator] But we two — ” “ Should be two dissatisfied ones linked together, which would be twice as bad as before … I think I should begin to be afraid of you, Jude, the moment you had contracted to cherish me under a Government stamp, and I was licensed to be loved on the premises by you — Ugh, how horrible and sordid! [/]
[I] Although, as you are, free, I trust you more than any other man in the world. ” [/]
[You] “ No, no — don’t say I should change! ” [/]
[Narrator] he expostulated; yet there was misgiving in his own voice also. [/]
[I] “ Apart from ourselves, and our unhappy peculiarities, it is foreign to a man ’s nature to go on loving a person when he is told that he must and shall be that person ’s lover. [/]
[I] There would be a much likelier chance of his doing it if he were told not to love. [/]
[I] If the marriage ceremony consisted in an oath and signed contract between the parties to cease loving from that day forward, in consideration of personal possession being given, and to avoid each other ’s society as much as possible in public, there would be more loving couples than there are now. [/]
[I] Fancy the secret meetings between the perjuring husband and wife, the denials of having seen each other, the clambering in at bedroom windows, and the hiding in closets! [/]
[I] There ’d be little cooling then. ” [/]
[Jude] “ Yes; but admitting this, or something like it, to be true, you are not the only one in the world to see it, dear little Sue. [/]
[Jude] People go on marrying because they ca n’t resist natural forces, although many of them may know perfectly well that they are possibly buying a month ’s pleasure with a life ’s discomfort. [/]

[Jude] A Nemesis attends the woman who plays the game of elusiveness too often, in the utter contempt for her that, sooner or later, her old admirers feel; under which they allow her to go unlamented to her grave. ” [/]
[Sue] Sue, who was regarding the distance, had acquired a guilty look; and she suddenly replied in a tragic voice: “ I do n’t think I like you to - day so well as I did, Jude! ” “ Do n’t you? [/]
[Sue] Why? ” “ Oh, well — you are not nice — too sermony. [/]

[Narrator] Sue was assisting Jude very materially now: he had latterly occupied himself on his own account in working and lettering headstones, which he kept in a little yard at the back of his little house, where in the intervals of domestic duties she marked out the letters full size for him, and blacked them in after he had cut them. [/]
[Jude] It was a lower class of handicraft than were his former performances as a cathedral mason, and his only patrons were the poor people who lived in his own neighbourhood, and knew what a cheap man this “ Jude Fawley: Monumental Mason ” (as he called himself on his front door) was to employ for the simple memorials they required for their dead. [/]

[Narrator] There is in Upper Wessex an old town of nine or ten thousand souls; the town may be called Stoke - Barehills. [/]
[Narrator] It stands with its gaunt, unattractive, ancient church, and its new red brick suburb, amid the open, chalk - soiled cornlands, near the middle of an imaginary triangle which has for its three corners the towns of Aldbrickham and Wintoncester, and the important military station of Quartershot. [/]
[Narrator] The great western highway from London passes through it, near a point where the road branches into two, merely to unite again some twenty miles further westward. [/]
[Narrator] Out of this bifurcation and reunion there used to arise among wheeled travellers, before railway days, endless questions of choice between the respective ways. [/]
[Narrator] But the question is now as dead as the scot - and - lot freeholder, the road waggoner, and the mail coachman who disputed it; and probably not a single inhabitant of Stoke - Barehills is now even aware that the two roads which part in his town ever meet again; for nobody now drives up and down the great western highway daily. [/]
[Narrator] The most familiar object in Stoke - Barehills nowadays is its cemetery, standing among some picturesque mediæval ruins beside the railway; the modern chapels, modern tombs, and modern shrubs having a look of intrusiveness amid the crumbling and ivy - covered decay of the ancient walls. [/]
[Narrator] On a certain day, however, in the particular year which has now been reached by this narrative — the month being early June — the features of the town excite little interest, though many visitors arrive by the trains; some down - trains, in especial, nearly emptying themselves here. [/]
[Narrator] It is the week of the Great Wessex Agricultural Show, whose vast encampment spreads over the open outskirts of the town like the tents of an investing army. [/]
[Narrator] Rows of marquees, huts, booths, pavilions, arcades, porticoes — every kind of structure short of a permanent one — cover the green field for the space of a square half - mile, and the crowds of arrivals walk through the town in a mass, and make straight for the exhibition ground. [/]
[Narrator] The way thereto is lined with shows, stalls, and hawkers on foot, who make a market - place of the whole roadway to the show proper, and lead some of the improvident to lighten their pockets appreciably before they reach the gates of the exhibition they came expressly to see. [/]
[Narrator] It is the popular day, the shilling day, and of the fast arriving excursion trains two from different directions enter the two contiguous railway stations at almost the same minute. [/]

[Narrator] The curious facts of a child coming to them unexpectedly, who called Jude “ Father, ” and Sue “ Mother, ” and a hitch in a marriage ceremony intended for quietness to be performed at a registrar ’s office, together with rumours of the undefended cases in the law - courts, bore only one translation to plain minds. [/]
[Jude] Little Time — for though he was formally turned into “ Jude, ” the apt nickname stuck to him — would come home from school in the evening, and repeat inquiries and remarks that had been made to him by the other boys; and cause Sue, and Jude when he heard them, a great deal of pain and sadness. [/]
[Narrator] The result was that shortly after the attempt at the registrar ’s the pair went off — to London it was believed — for several days, hiring somebody to look to the boy. [/]
[Narrator] When they came back they let it be understood indirectly, and with total indifference and weariness of mien, that they were legally married at last. [/]
[Narrator] Sue, who had previously been called Mrs. Bridehead now openly adopted the name of Mrs. Fawley. [/]
[Narrator] Her dull, cowed, and listless manner for days seemed to substantiate all this. [/]
[Narrator] But the mistake (as it was called) of their going away so secretly to do the business, kept up much of the mystery of their lives; and they found that they made not such advances with their neighbours as they had expected to do thereby. [/]
[Narrator] A living mystery was not much less interesting than a dead scandal. [/]
[Narrator] The baker ’s lad and the grocer ’s boy, who at first had used to lift their hats gallantly to Sue when they came to execute their errands, in these days no longer took the trouble to render her that homage, and the neighbouring artizans ’ wives looked straight along the pavement when they encountered her. [/]
[Narrator] Nobody molested them, it is true; but an oppressive atmosphere began to encircle their souls, particularly after their excursion to the show, as if that visit had brought some evil influence to bear on them. [/]
[Narrator] And their temperaments were precisely of a kind to suffer from this atmosphere, and to be indisposed to lighten it by vigorous and open statements. [/]
[Narrator] Their apparent attempt at reparation had come too late to be effective. [/]
[Narrator] The headstone and epitaph orders fell off: and two or three months later, when autumn came, Jude perceived that he would have to return to journey - work again, a course all the more unfortunate just now, in that he had not as yet cleared off the debt he had unavoidably incurred in the payment of the law - costs of the previous year. [/]
[Narrator] One evening he sat down to share the common meal with Sue and the child as usual. [/]
[Jude] “ I am thinking, ” he said to her, “ that I ’ll hold on here no longer. [/]
[Jude] The life suits us, certainly; but if we could get away to a place where we are unknown, we should be lighter hearted, and have a better chance. [/]
[Jude] And so I am afraid we must break it up here, however awkward for you, poor dear! ” [/]
[Narrator] Sue was always much affected at a picture of herself as an object of pity, and she saddened. [/]
[Sue] “ Well — I am not sorry, ” said she presently. [/]
[Sue] “ I am much depressed by the way they look at me here. [/]
[Sue] And you have been keeping on this house and furniture entirely for me and the boy! [/]
[Sue] You do n’t want it yourself, and the expense is unnecessary. [/]
[Sue] But whatever we do, wherever we go, you wo n’t take him away from me, Jude dear? [/]
[Sue] I could not let him go now! [/]
[Sue] The cloud upon his young mind makes him so pathetic to me; I do hope to lift it some day! [/]
[Sue] And he loves me so. [/]
[Narrator] You wo n’t take him away from me? ” “ Certainly I wo n’t, dear little girl! [/]
[Jude] We ’ll get nice lodgings, wherever we go. [/]
[Jude] I shall be moving about probably — getting a job here and a job there. ” [/]
[Narrator] “ I shall do something too, of course, till — till — Well, now I ca n’t be useful in the lettering it behoves me to turn my hand to something else. ” “ Do n’t hurry about getting employment, ” he said regretfully. [/]
[I] “ I do n’t want you to do that. [/]
[I] I wish you would n’t, Sue. [/]
[I] The boy and yourself are enough for you to attend to. ” [/]
[Narrator] There was a knock at the door, and Jude answered it. [/]
[Sue] Sue could hear the conversation: “ Is Mr. Fawley at home? … [/]
[Sue] Biles and Willis, the building contractors, sent me to know if you ’ll undertake the relettering of the ten commandments in a little church they ’ve been restoring lately in the country near here. ” [/]
[Narrator] Jude reflected, and said he could undertake it. [/]
[TheMessenger] “ It is not a very artistic job, ” continued the messenger. [/]
[TheMessenger] “ The clergyman is a very old - fashioned chap, and he has refused to let anything more be done to the church than cleaning and repairing. ” [/]
[Sue] “ Excellent old man! ” said Sue to herself, who was sentimentally opposed to the horrors of over - restoration. [/]
[TheMessenger] “ The Ten Commandments are fixed to the east end, ” the messenger went on, “ and they want doing up with the rest of the wall there, since he wo n’t have them carted off as old materials belonging to the contractor in the usual way of the trade. ” [/]
[Narrator] A bargain as to terms was struck, and Jude came indoors. [/]
[Jude] “ There, you see, ” he said cheerfully. [/]
[Jude] “ One more job yet, at any rate, and you can help in it — at least you can try. [/]
[Jude] We shall have all the church to ourselves, as the rest of the work is finished. ” [/]
[Narrator] Next day Jude went out to the church, which was only two miles off. [/]
[Narrator] He found that what the contractor ’s clerk had said was true. [/]
[Narrator] The tables of the Jewish law towered sternly over the utensils of Christian grace, as the chief ornament of the chancel end, in the fine dry style of the last century. [/]
[Narrator] And as their framework was constructed of ornamental plaster they could not be taken down for repair. [/]
[Narrator] A portion, crumbled by damp, required renewal; and when this had been done, and the whole cleansed, he began to renew the lettering. [/]
[Narrator] On the second morning Sue came to see what assistance she could render, and also because they liked to be together. [/]
[Narrator] The silence and emptiness of the building gave her confidence, and, standing on a safe low platform erected by Jude, which she was nevertheless timid at mounting, she began painting in the letters of the first Table while he set about mending a portion of the second. [/]
[Narrator] She was quite pleased at her powers; she had acquired them in the days she painted illumined texts for the church - fitting shop at Christminster. [/]
[Narrator] Nobody seemed likely to disturb them; and the pleasant twitter of birds, and rustle of October leafage, came in through an open window, and mingled with their talk. [/]
[Narrator] They were not, however, to be left thus snug and peaceful for long. [/]
[Narrator] About half - past twelve there came footsteps on the gravel without. [/]
[Narrator] The old vicar and his churchwarden entered, and, coming up to see what was being done, seemed surprised to discover that a young woman was assisting. [/]
[Narrator] They passed on into an aisle, at which time the door again opened, and another figure entered — a small one, that of little Time, who was crying. [/]
[Narrator] Sue had told him where he might find her between school - hours, if he wished. [/]
[Narrator] She came down from her perch, and said, “ What ’s the matter, my dear? ” “ I could n’t stay to eat my dinner in school, because they said — ” He described how some boys had taunted him about his nominal mother, and Sue, grieved, expressed her indignation to Jude aloft. [/]
[Narrator] The child went into the churchyard, and Sue returned to her work. [/]
[Narrator] Meanwhile the door had opened again, and there shuffled in with a businesslike air the white - aproned woman who cleaned the church. [/]
[Narrator] Sue recognized her as one who had friends in Spring Street, whom she visited. [/]
[Narrator] The church - cleaner looked at Sue, gaped, and lifted her hands; she had evidently recognized Jude ’s companion as the latter had recognized her. [/]
[Narrator] Next came two ladies, and after talking to the charwoman they also moved forward, and as Sue stood reaching upward, watched her hand tracing the letters, and critically regarded her person in relief against the white wall, till she grew so nervous that she trembled visibly. [/]
[Narrator] They went back to where the others were standing, talking in undertones: and one said — Sue could not hear which—“She ’s his wife, I suppose? ” “ Some say Yes: some say No, ” was the reply from the charwoman. [/]
[One] “ Not? [/]
[One] Then she ought to be, or somebody’s — that ’s very clear! ” “ They ’ve only been married a very few weeks, whether or no. ” [/]
[One] “ A strange pair to be painting the Two Tables! [/]
[One] I wonder Biles and Willis could think of such a thing as hiring those! ” [/]
[Narrator] The churchwarden supposed that Biles and Willis knew of nothing wrong, and then the other, who had been talking to the old woman, explained what she meant by calling them strange people. [/]
[TheChurchwarden] The probable drift of the subdued conversation which followed was made plain by the churchwarden breaking into an anecdote, in a voice that everybody in the church could hear, though obviously suggested by the present situation: “ Well, now, it is a curious thing, but my grandfather told me a strange tale of a most immoral case that happened at the painting of the Commandments in a church out by Gaymead — which is quite within a walk of this one. [/]
[TheChurchwarden] In them days Commandments were mostly done in gilt letters on a black ground, and that ’s how they were out where I say, before the owld church was rebuilded. [/]
[TheChurchwarden] It must have been somewhere about a hundred years ago that them Commandments wanted doing up just as ours do here, and they had to get men from Aldbrickham to do ’em. [/]
[TheChurchwarden] Now they wished to get the job finished by a particular Sunday, so the men had to work late Saturday night, against their will, for overtime was not paid then as ’ tis now. [/]
[TheChurchwarden] There was no true religion in the country at that date, neither among pa’sons, clerks, nor people, and to keep the men up to their work the vicar had to let ’em have plenty of drink during the afternoon. [/]
[TheChurchwarden] As evening drawed on they sent for some more themselves; rum, by all account. [/]
[TheChurchwarden] It got later and later, and they got more and more fuddled, till at last they went a - putting their rum - bottle and rummers upon the communion table, and drawed up a trestle or two, and sate round comfortable and poured out again right hearty bumpers. [/]
[TheChurchwarden] No sooner had they tossed off their glasses than, so the story goes, they fell down senseless, one and all. [/]
[TheChurchwarden] How long they bode so they did n’t know, but when they came to themselves there was a terrible thunder - storm a - raging, and they seemed to see in the gloom a dark figure with very thin legs and a curious voot, a - standing on the ladder, and finishing their work. [/]
[TheChurchwarden] When it got daylight they could see that the work was really finished, and could n’t at all mind finishing it themselves. [/]
[TheChurchwarden] They went home, and the next thing they heard was that a great scandal had been caused in the church that Sunday morning, for when the people came and service began, all saw that the Ten Commandments wez painted with the ‘ nots ’ left out. [/]
[TheChurchwarden] Decent people would n’t attend service there for a long time, and the Bishop had to be sent for to reconsecrate the church. [/]
[TheChurchwarden] That ’s the tradition as I used to hear it as a child. [/]
[TheChurchwarden] You must take it for what it is wo’th, but this case to - day has reminded me o’t, as I say. ” [/]
[TheVisitors] The visitors gave one more glance, as if to see whether Jude and Sue had left the “ nots ” out likewise, and then severally left the church, even the old woman at last. [/]
[Narrator] Sue and Jude, who had not stopped working, sent back the child to school, and remained without speaking; till, looking at her narrowly, he found she had been crying silently. [/]
[Jude] “ Never mind, comrade! ” [/]
[Narrator] he said. [/]
[Jude] “ I know what it is! ” “ I ca n’t _ bear _ that they, and everybody, should think people wicked because they may have chosen to live their own way! [/]

[Jude] It was only a funny story. ” “ Ah, but we suggested it! [/]
[Jude] I am afraid I have done you mischief, Jude, instead of helping you by coming! ” [/]
[Narrator] To have suggested such a story was certainly not very exhilarating, in a serious view of their position. [/]
[Narrator] However, in a few minutes Sue seemed to see that their position this morning had a ludicrous side, and wiping her eyes she laughed. [/]
[Sue] “ It is droll, after all, ” she said, “ that we two, of all people, with our queer history, should happen to be here painting the Ten Commandments! [/]

[Narrator] They sat down to lunch — which they had brought with them not to hinder time — and having eaten it, were about to set to work anew when a man entered the church, and Jude recognized in him the contractor Willis. [/]
[Narrator] He beckoned to Jude, and spoke to him apart. [/]

[Narrator] Jude was too independent to make any fuss; and the contractor paid him, and left. [/]

[Narrator] said she, dropping to her tragic note. [/]

[Narrator] They sat down passively for a few minutes, proceeded out of the church, and overtaking the boy pursued their thoughtful way to Aldbrickham. [/]
[AnArtizansMutualImprovementSocietyEstablishedInTheTown] Fawley had still a pretty zeal in the cause of education, and, as was natural with his experiences, he was active in furthering “ equality of opportunity ” by any humble means open to him. [/]
[Narrator] He had joined an Artizans ’ Mutual Improvement Society established in the town about the time of his arrival there; its members being young men of all creeds and denominations, including Churchmen, Congregationalists, Baptists, Unitarians, Positivists, and others — Agnostics had scarcely been heard of at this time — their one common wish to enlarge their minds forming a sufficiently close bond of union. [/]
[Narrator] The subscription was small, and the room homely; and Jude ’s activity, uncustomary acquirements, and, above all, singular intuition on what to read and how to set about it — begotten of his years of struggle against malignant stars — had led to his being placed on the committee. [/]
[Narrator] A few evenings after his dismissal from the church repairs, and before he had obtained any more work to do, he went to attend a meeting of the aforesaid committee. [/]
[Narrator] It was late when he arrived: all the others had come, and as he entered they looked dubiously at him, and hardly uttered a word of greeting. [/]
[Narrator] He guessed that something bearing on himself had been either discussed or mooted. [/]
[Narrator] Some ordinary business was transacted, and it was disclosed that the number of subscriptions had shown a sudden falling off for that quarter. [/]
[Narrator] One member — a really well - meaning and upright man — began speaking in enigmas about certain possible causes: that it behoved them to look well into their constitution; for if the committee were not respected, and had not at least, in their differences, a common standard of _ conduct _, they would bring the institution to the ground. [/]
[Narrator] Nothing further was said in Jude ’s presence, but he knew what this meant; and turning to the table wrote a note resigning his office there and then. [/]
[Narrator] Thus the supersensitive couple were more and more impelled to go away. [/]
[Narrator] And then bills were sent in, and the question arose, what could Jude do with his great - aunt ’s heavy old furniture, if he left the town to travel he knew not whither? [/]
[Narrator] This, and the necessity of ready money, compelled him to decide on an auction, much as he would have preferred to keep the venerable goods. [/]
[Narrator] The day of the sale came on; and Sue for the last time cooked her own, the child ’s, and Jude ’s breakfast in the little house he had furnished. [/]
[Narrator] It chanced to be a wet day; moreover Sue was unwell, and not wishing to desert her poor Jude in such gloomy circumstances, for he was compelled to stay awhile, she acted on the suggestion of the auctioneer ’s man, and ensconced herself in an upper room, which could be emptied of its effects, and so kept closed to the bidders. [/]
[Narrator] Here Jude discovered her; and with the child, and their few trunks, baskets, and bundles, and two chairs and a table that were not in the sale, the two sat in meditative talk. [/]
[Narrator] Footsteps began stamping up and down the bare stairs, the comers inspecting the goods, some of which were of so quaint and ancient a make as to acquire an adventitious value as art. [/]
[Jude] Their door was tried once or twice, and to guard themselves against intrusion Jude wrote “ Private ” on a scrap of paper, and stuck it upon the panel. [/]
[Narrator] They soon found that, instead of the furniture, their own personal histories and past conduct began to be discussed to an unexpected and intolerable extent by the intending bidders. [/]
[Narrator] It was not till now that they really discovered what a fools ’ paradise of supposed unrecognition they had been living in of late. [/]
[Narrator] Sue silently took her companion ’s hand, and with eyes on each other they heard these passing remarks — the quaint and mysterious personality of Father Time being a subject which formed a large ingredient in the hints and innuendoes. [/]
[Narrator] At length the auction began in the room below, whence they could hear each familiar article knocked down, the highly prized ones cheaply, the unconsidered at an unexpected price. [/]
[Jude] “ People do n’t understand us, ” he sighed heavily. [/]
[Narrator] “ I am glad we have decided to go. ” “ The question is, where to? ” “ It ought to be to London. [/]
[Jude] There one can live as one chooses. ” [/]
[TheAuctioneer] “ No — not London, dear! [/]
[TheAuctioneer] I know it well. [/]
[TheAuctioneer] We should be unhappy there. ” [/]
[Narrator] “ Why? ” “ Ca n’t you think? ” “ Because Arabella is there? ” “ That ’s the chief reason. ” [/]

[Jude] To cut him off from his past I have determined to keep silence. [/]
[Narrator] I am sickened of ecclesiastical work now; and I should n’t like to accept it, if offered me! ” “ You ought to have learnt classic. [/]
[TheAuctioneer] Gothic is barbaric art, after all. [/]

[Narrator] “ Unless he keeps a cake and gingerbread stall at markets and fairs, where people are gloriously indifferent to everything except the quality of the goods. ” Their thoughts were diverted by the voice of the auctioneer: “ Now this antique oak settle — a unique example of old English furniture, worthy the attention of all collectors! ” “ That was my great - grandfather ’s, ” said Jude. [/]
[Jude] “ I wish we could have kept the poor old thing! ” [/]
[Narrator] One by one the articles went, and the afternoon passed away. [/]
[Narrator] Jude and the other two were getting tired and hungry, but after the conversation they had heard they were shy of going out while the purchasers were in their line of retreat. [/]
[Narrator] However, the later lots drew on, and it became necessary to emerge into the rain soon, to take on Sue ’s things to their temporary lodging. [/]
[Sue] “ Now the next lot: two pairs of pigeons, all alive and plump — a nice pie for somebody for next Sunday ’s dinner! ” [/]
[Narrator] The impending sale of these birds had been the most trying suspense of the whole afternoon. [/]
[Narrator] They were Sue ’s pets, and when it was found that they could not possibly be kept, more sadness was caused than by parting from all the furniture. [/]
[Narrator] Sue tried to think away her tears as she heard the trifling sum that her dears were deemed to be worth advanced by small stages to the price at which they were finally knocked down. [/]
[Narrator] The purchaser was a neighbouring poulterer, and they were unquestionably doomed to die before the next market day. [/]
[Narrator] Noting her dissembled distress Jude kissed her, and said it was time to go and see if the lodgings were ready. [/]
[Narrator] He would go on with the boy, and fetch her soon. [/]
[Narrator] When she was left alone she waited patiently, but Jude did not come back. [/]
[Narrator] At last she started, the coast being clear, and on passing the poulterer ’s shop, not far off, she saw her pigeons in a hamper by the door. [/]
[Narrator] An emotion at sight of them, assisted by the growing dusk of evening, caused her to act on impulse, and first looking around her quickly, she pulled out the peg which fastened down the cover, and went on. [/]
[Narrator] The cover was lifted from within, and the pigeons flew away with a clatter that brought the chagrined poulterer cursing and swearing to the door. [/]
[Narrator] Sue reached the lodging trembling, and found Jude and the boy making it comfortable for her. [/]

[Narrator] she asked breathlessly. [/]

[Narrator] “ As soon as the sale - account is wound up, and our bills paid, we go. ” “ Where do we go to? ” [/]
[Narrator] asked Time, in suspense. [/]
[Jude] “ We must sail under sealed orders, that nobody may trace us … We must n’t go to Alfredston, or to Melchester, or to Shaston, or to Christminster. [/]
[Jude] Apart from those we may go anywhere. ” “ Why must n’t we go there, Father? ” “ Because of a cloud that has gathered over us; though ‘ we have wronged no man, corrupted no man, defrauded no man! ’ [/]
[Jude] Though perhaps we have ‘ done that which was right in our own eyes. ’ ” VII From that week Jude Fawley and Sue walked no more in the town of Aldbrickham. [/]
[Narrator] Whither they had gone nobody knew, chiefly because nobody cared to know. [/]
[Narrator] Any one sufficiently curious to trace the steps of such an obscure pair might have discovered without great trouble that they had taken advantage of his adaptive craftsmanship to enter on a shifting, almost nomadic, life, which was not without its pleasantness for a time. [/]
[Narrator] Wherever Jude heard of free - stone work to be done, thither he went, choosing by preference places remote from his old haunts and Sue ’s. [/]
[Narrator] He laboured at a job, long or briefly, till it was finished; and then moved on. [/]
[Narrator] Two whole years and a half passed thus. [/]
[Narrator] Sometimes he might have been found shaping the mullions of a country mansion, sometimes setting the parapet of a town - hall, sometimes ashlaring an hotel at Sandbourne, sometimes a museum at Casterbridge, sometimes as far down as Exonbury, sometimes at Stoke - Barehills. [/]
[Narrator] Later still he was at Kennetbridge, a thriving town not more than a dozen miles south of Marygreen, this being his nearest approach to the village where he was known; for he had a sensitive dread of being questioned as to his life and fortunes by those who had been acquainted with him during his ardent young manhood of study and promise, and his brief and unhappy married life at that time. [/]
[Narrator] At some of these places he would be detained for months, at others only a few weeks. [/]
[Narrator] His curious and sudden antipathy to ecclesiastical work, both episcopal and noncomformist, which had risen in him when suffering under a smarting sense of misconception, remained with him in cold blood, less from any fear of renewed censure than from an ultra - conscientiousness which would not allow him to seek a living out of those who would disapprove of his ways; also, too, from a sense of inconsistency between his former dogmas and his present practice, hardly a shred of the beliefs with which he had first gone up to Christminster now remaining with him. [/]
[Narrator] He was mentally approaching the position which Sue had occupied when he first met her. [/]
[Narrator] On a Saturday evening in May, nearly three years after Arabella ’s recognition of Sue and himself at the agricultural show, some of those who there encountered each other met again. [/]
[Narrator] It was the spring fair at Kennetbridge, and, though this ancient trade - meeting had much dwindled from its dimensions of former times, the long straight street of the borough presented a lively scene about midday. [/]

[Narrator] I On their arrival the station was lively with straw - hatted young men, welcoming young girls who bore a remarkable family likeness to their welcomers, and who were dressed up in the brightest and lightest of raiment. [/]
[Sue] “ The place seems gay, ” said Sue. [/]

[Jude] We can get them afterwards. ” [/]

[Narrator] “ I am sure this sight will awaken old sorrows in you, and do no good! ” “ Well — we are near; we will see it now, ” said he. [/]
[Narrator] They turned in on the left by the church with the Italian porch, whose helical columns were heavily draped with creepers, and pursued the lane till there arose on Jude ’s sight the circular theatre with that well - known lantern above it, which stood in his mind as the sad symbol of his abandoned hopes, for it was from that outlook that he had finally surveyed the City of Colleges on the afternoon of his great meditation, which convinced him at last of the futility of his attempt to be a son of the university. [/]
[Narrator] To - day, in the open space stretching between this building and the nearest college, stood a crowd of expectant people. [/]
[Narrator] A passage was kept clear through their midst by two barriers of timber, extending from the door of the college to the door of the large building between it and the theatre. [/]
[Jude] “ Here is the place — they are just going to pass! ” cried Jude in sudden excitement. [/]
[Narrator] And pushing his way to the front he took up a position close to the barrier, still hugging the youngest child in his arms, while Sue and the others kept immediately behind him. [/]
[Narrator] The crowd filled in at their back, and fell to talking, joking, and laughing as carriage after carriage drew up at the lower door of the college, and solemn stately figures in blood - red robes began to alight. [/]
[Narrator] The sky had grown overcast and livid, and thunder rumbled now and then. [/]
[Narrator] Father Time shuddered. [/]
[Jude] “ It do seem like the Judgment Day! ” [/]
[Narrator] he whispered. [/]
[Sue] “ They are only learned Doctors, ” said Sue. While they waited big drops of rain fell on their heads and shoulders, and the delay grew tedious. [/]
[Narrator] Sue again wished not to stay. [/]
[Jude] “ They wo n’t be long now, ” said Jude, without turning his head. [/]
[Narrator] But the procession did not come forth, and somebody in the crowd, to pass the time, looked at the façade of the nearest college, and said he wondered what was meant by the Latin inscription in its midst. [/]
[Narrator] Jude, who stood near the inquirer, explained it, and finding that the people all round him were listening with interest, went on to describe the carving of the frieze (which he had studied years before), and to criticize some details of masonry in other college fronts about the city. [/]
[OneOfThem] The idle crowd, including the two policemen at the doors, stared like the Lycaonians at Paul, for Jude was apt to get too enthusiastic over any subject in hand, and they seemed to wonder how the stranger should know more about the buildings of their town than they themselves did; till one of them said: “ Why, I know that man; he used to work here years ago — Jude Fawley, that ’s his name! [Narrator] Jude replied good - humouredly that he could not dispute it; and from remark to remark something like a general conversation arose between him and the crowd of idlers, during which Tinker Taylor asked Jude if he remembered the Apostles ’ Creed in Latin still, and the night of the challenge in the public house. [/]

[Narrator] murmured little Time mournfully, as he stood submerged and invisible in the crowd. [/]
[Sue] But finding himself the centre of curiosity, quizzing, and comment, Jude was not inclined to shrink from open declarations of what he had no great reason to be ashamed of; and in a little while was stimulated to say in a loud voice to the listening throng generally: “ It is a difficult question, my friends, for any young man — that question I had to grapple with, and which thousands are weighing at the present moment in these uprising times — whether to follow uncritically the track he finds himself in, without considering his aptness for it, or to consider what his aptness or bent may be, and re - shape his course accordingly. [/]
[Sue] I tried to do the latter, and I failed. [/]
[Sue] But I do n’t admit that my failure proved my view to be a wrong one, or that my success would have made it a right one; though that ’s how we appraise such attempts nowadays — I mean, not by their essential soundness, but by their accidental outcomes. [/]
[Sue] If I had ended by becoming like one of these gentlemen in red and black that we saw dropping in here by now, everybody would have said: ‘ See how wise that young man was, to follow the bent of his nature! ’ [/]
[Sue] But having ended no better than I began they say: ‘ See what a fool that fellow was in following a freak of his fancy! ’ [/]
[Sue] “ However it was my poverty and not my will that consented to be beaten. [/]
[Sue] It takes two or three generations to do what I tried to do in one; and my impulses — affections — vices perhaps they should be called — were too strong not to hamper a man without advantages; who should be as cold - blooded as a fish and as selfish as a pig to have a really good chance of being one of his country ’s worthies. [/]
[Sue] You may ridicule me — I am quite willing that you should — I am a fit subject, no doubt. [/]
[Sue] But I think if you knew what I have gone through these last few years you would rather pity me. [/]
[Sue] And if they knew”—he nodded towards the college at which the dons were severally arriving—“it is just possible they would do the same. ” [/]
[AWoman] “ He do look ill and worn - out, it is true! ” said a woman. [/]
[Narrator] Sue ’s face grew more emotional; but though she stood close to Jude she was screened. [/]

[Narrator] “ I was, perhaps, after all, a paltry victim to the spirit of mental and social restlessness that makes so many unhappy in these days! ” “ Do n’t tell them that! ” whispered Sue with tears, at perceiving Jude ’s state of mind. [/]
[Sue] “ You were n’t that. [/]

[Narrator] Sue hesitated; and then impulsively told the woman that her husband and herself had each been unhappy in their first marriages, after which, terrified at the thought of a second irrevocable union, and lest the conditions of the contract should kill their love, yet wishing to be together, they had literally not found the courage to repeat it, though they had attempted it two or three times. [/]

[Narrator] However, there was no doubt that the newspaper report was in some way true. [/]
[Gillingham] “ Their cup of sorrow is now full! ” [/]
[Narrator] he said: and thought and thought of Sue, and what she had gained by leaving him. [/]
[Narrator] Arabella having made her home at Alfredston, and the schoolmaster coming to market there every Saturday, it was not wonderful that in a few weeks they met again — the precise time being just after her return from Christminster, where she had stayed much longer than she had at first intended, keeping an interested eye on Jude, though Jude had seen no more of her. [/]
[Narrator] Phillotson was on his way homeward when he encountered Arabella, and she was approaching the town. [/]
[Gillingham] “ You like walking out this way, Mrs. Cartlett? ” he said. [/]
[Arabella] “ I ’ve just begun to again, ” she replied. [/]
[Arabella] “ It is where I lived as maid and wife, and all the past things of my life that are interesting to my feelings are mixed up with this road. [/]

[Narrator] Yes; I ’ve seen Jude. ” “ Ah! [/]

[Narrator] Arabella gave it. [/]
[Arabella] “ Thank you. [/]

[Narrator] Arabella smiled grimly as she resumed her way, and practised dimple - making all along the road from where the pollard willows begin to the old almshouses in the first street of the town. [/]
[Narrator] Meanwhile Phillotson ascended to Marygreen, and for the first time during a lengthened period he lived with a forward eye. [/]
[Narrator] On crossing under the large trees of the green to the humble schoolhouse to which he had been reduced he stood a moment, and pictured Sue coming out of the door to meet him. [/]
[Narrator] No man had ever suffered more inconvenience from his own charity, Christian or heathen, than Phillotson had done in letting Sue go. [/]
[Narrator] He had been knocked about from pillar to post at the hands of the virtuous almost beyond endurance; he had been nearly starved, and was now dependent entirely upon the very small stipend from the school of this village (where the parson had got ill - spoken of for befriending him). [/]
[Narrator] He had often thought of Arabella ’s remarks that he should have been more severe with Sue, that her recalcitrant spirit would soon have been broken. [/]
[Narrator] Yet such was his obstinate and illogical disregard of opinion, and of the principles in which he had been trained, that his convictions on the rightness of his course with his wife had not been disturbed. [/]
[Narrator] Principles which could be subverted by feeling in one direction were liable to the same catastrophe in another. [/]
[Narrator] The instincts which had allowed him to give Sue her liberty now enabled him to regard her as none the worse for her life with Jude. [/]
[Narrator] He wished for her still, in his curious way, if he did not love her, and, apart from policy, soon felt that he would be gratified to have her again as his, always provided that she came willingly. [/]
[Narrator] But artifice was necessary, he had found, for stemming the cold and inhumane blast of the world ’s contempt. [/]
[Narrator] And here were the materials ready made. [/]
[Narrator] By getting Sue back and remarrying her on the respectable plea of having entertained erroneous views of her, and gained his divorce wrongfully, he might acquire some comfort, resume his old courses, perhaps return to the Shaston school, if not even to the Church as a licentiate. [/]
[Narrator] He thought he would write to Gillingham to inquire his views, and what he thought of his, Phillotson ’s, sending a letter to her. [/]
[Narrator] Gillingham replied, naturally, that now she was gone it were best to let her be, and considered that if she were anybody ’s wife she was the wife of the man to whom she had borne three children and owed such tragical adventures. [/]
[Narrator] Probably, as his attachment to her seemed unusually strong, the singular pair would make their union legal in course of time, and all would be well, and decent, and in order. [/]
[Phillotson] “ But they won’t — Sue wo n’t! ” [/]
[Narrator] exclaimed Phillotson to himself. [/]
[Phillotson] “ Gillingham is so matter of fact. [/]
[Phillotson] She ’s affected by Christminster sentiment and teaching. [/]

[Phillotson] They are not mine; but I shall make use of them to further mine. ” [/]
[Narrator] He wrote a brief reply to Gillingham. [/]
[He] “ I know I am entirely wrong, but I do n’t agree with you. [/]
[He] As to her having lived with and had three children by him, my feeling is (though I can advance no logical or moral defence of it, on the old lines) that it has done little more than finish her education. [/]
[He] I shall write to her, and learn whether what that woman said is true or no. ” [/]
[Narrator] As he had made up his mind to do this before he had written to his friend, there had not been much reason for writing to the latter at all. [/]
[Narrator] However, it was Phillotson ’s way to act thus. [/]
[Narrator] He accordingly addressed a carefully considered epistle to Sue, and, knowing her emotional temperament, threw a Rhadamanthine strictness into the lines here and there, carefully hiding his heterodox feelings, not to frighten her. [/]
[Narrator] He stated that, it having come to his knowledge that her views had considerably changed, he felt compelled to say that his own, too, were largely modified by events subsequent to their parting. [/]
[Narrator] He would not conceal from her that passionate love had little to do with his communication. [/]
[Narrator] It arose from a wish to make their lives, if not a success, at least no such disastrous failure as they threatened to become, through his acting on what he had considered at the time a principle of justice, charity, and reason. [/]
[Narrator] To indulge one ’s instinctive and uncontrolled sense of justice and right, was not, he had found, permitted with impunity in an old civilization like ours. [/]
[Narrator] It was necessary to act under an acquired and cultivated sense of the same, if you wished to enjoy an average share of comfort and honour; and to let crude loving kindness take care of itself. [/]
[Narrator] He suggested that she should come to him there at Marygreen. [/]
[Narrator] On second thoughts he took out the last paragraph but one; and having rewritten the letter he dispatched it immediately, and in some excitement awaited the issue. [/]
[Narrator] A few days after a figure moved through the white fog which enveloped the Beersheba suburb of Christminster, towards the quarter in which Jude Fawley had taken up his lodging since his division from Sue. [/]

[Narrator] There are cold spots up and down Wessex in autumn and winter weather; but the coldest of all when a north or east wind is blowing is the crest of the down by the Brown House, where the road to Alfredston crosses the old Ridgeway. [/]

[Narrator] His face was now so thin that his old friends would hardly have known him. [/]
[Narrator] It was afternoon, and Arabella was at the looking - glass curling her hair, which operation she performed by heating an umbrella - stay in the flame of a candle she had lighted, and using it upon the flowing lock. [/]

[Narrator] The house was empty. [/]

[Narrator] It was a warm, cloudless, enticing day. [/]
